1.4 Full-context measurements

We fill each user’s context to check GPU memory. Smaller contexts with the same settings should fit with more room. These are verified limits, not exact maximums.

Shared settings:

  • Context target per user: E4B 131,072; Gemma 12B and Qwen 262,144
  • Flash Attention (FA): on
  • Unified KV cache: on
  • Thinking: off
  • MTP: up to three draft tokens; four tokens reserved per user when enabled

Pass: every user fills the context and generates 128 tokens, with at least 512 MiB GPU spare, no dropped input, prompt-cache reuse or context shifting.

Allocated / usable means reserved space / input and output space. GPU memory is measured unless marked calculated; calculated values are minimum requirements. — means unavailable.

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This image depicts a section of the **Great Wall of China**.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast network of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical purpose was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions by nomadic groups from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous and extensive sections, like the one likely pictured, were largely constructed during the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644).

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous defensive walls built along the ridge.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortifications:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built into the wall, used for observation, defense, and housing troops.
*   **Stairways/Access Points:** Steep, stone-built staircases leading up to and along the wall, allowing for troop movement and surveillance.
*   **Stone/Brick Construction:** The primary building material visible, characteristic of later Ming Dynasty construction, providing durability against the elements and attackers.

Finish: stop

This image depicts a section of the **Great Wall of China**.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast system of fortifications built, rebuilt, and maintained by various Chinese dynasties over many centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical purpose was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions by various nomadic groups from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous and well-preserved sections, like the one shown, date primarily to the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644).

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous defensive walls constructed of stone and brick.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortifications:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built into the wall line, used for observation, defense, and housing troops.
*   **Stairways/Access Points:** Steep, fortified staircases are visible, allowing movement along the wall and up to the towers.
*   **Battlements/Parapets:** The top sections of the walls, designed to provide cover for defenders.
*   **Material:** The construction utilizes large amounts of brick and stone, characteristic of later imperial building techniques.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast system of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over many centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical purpose was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions, particularly from nomadic groups of the Eurasian Steppe. The construction spanned numerous dynasties, with significant building and reinforcement occurring during the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644), which is when much of the well-preserved stone and brickwork seen today was constructed.

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Ramparts/Curtain Walls:** Long, imposing defensive walls constructed primarily of stone and brick, forming the main defensive barrier.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortifications:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built into the wall at intervals. These served as garrisons, observation posts, and signaling stations.
*   **Stairways/Ascending Paths:** Steep, constructed staircases are visible, allowing defenders and troops to move quickly along the wall and up to the towers.
*   **Battlements/Parapets:** The top edge of the wall features defensive parapets, providing cover for soldiers.
*   **Integration with Terrain:** The wall is strategically built to follow and utilize the natural contours of the mountainous landscape.

This image depicts a section of the **Great Wall of China**.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast network of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over many centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical function was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions from nomadic groups of various peoples from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous and extensive sections seen today were largely constructed during the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644).

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Ramparts/Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous defensive walls built along the ridge.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortified Towers:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built into the wall at regular intervals, used for observation, signaling, and defense.
*   **Stairways/Access Points:** Steep, stone-built staircases leading up to the wall, allowing defenders and travelers access to the ramparts.
*   **Stone Masonry:** The construction utilizes durable stone and brickwork, characteristic of later Ming Dynasty fortifications.
*   **Natural Integration:** The wall is strategically built to follow and utilize the natural contours of the mountainous terrain for maximum defensive advantage.

ChineseWall · 97.00%

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This image depicts a section of the **Great Wall of China**.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast network of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over many centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical function was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions from nomadic groups of various peoples from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous and well-preserved sections visible today often date back to the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644), which undertook massive reconstruction and reinforcement projects.

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous defensive walls constructed along the ridge.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortifications:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built into the wall line, used for observation, defense, and housing soldiers.
*   **Stairways/Access Paths:** Steep, fortified staircases are visible, providing access to the different levels of the wall and towers.
*   **Ramparts:** The top walkway of the wall, designed for troop movement and defense.
*   **Stone/Brick Construction:** The primary building material visible is large, durable stone and brickwork.

Finish: stop

clarity: extensive feature inventory repeats visible details and is longer than requested.

Configurations: gemma-e4b-Q4_0-vulkan-n1-mtp0-q8_0

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast network of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over many centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical function was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions, particularly from nomadic groups of the Eurasian Steppe. The construction spanned numerous dynasties, with significant building and reinforcement occurring during the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644), which is when much of the well-preserved stone and brickwork seen today was constructed.

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous defensive walls made of stone and brick that form the main barrier.

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This image depicts a section of the **Great Wall of China**.

**History:**
The Great Wall is not a single continuous structure but a vast network of fortifications built and rebuilt by various Chinese dynasties over centuries, beginning as early as the 7th century BC. Its primary historical purpose was to protect Chinese states and empires against raids and invasions from nomadic groups of various peoples from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous and extensive sections visible today were largely constructed or reinforced during the Ming Dynasty (1368–1644).

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Curtain Walls:** The main defensive structure, characterized by high, thick stone or brick walls forming a continuous barrier.
*   **Watchtowers/Fortifications:** Prominent, square or rectangular structures built along the wall (like the larger structures visible) that served as defensive strongholds, barracks, and observation posts.
*   **Stairways/Ascending Paths:** Steep, constructed staircases are visible, allowing passage up and down the steep, rugged terrain.
*   **Construction Material:** The walls appear to be constructed primarily of brick and stone, typical of later Ming Dynasty fortifications.
*   **Integration with Landscape:** The wall is strategically built to follow and utilize the natural contours of the mountainous terrain for maximum defensive advantage.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a historic fortification system built across the northern borders of China.

**History:**
The wall was constructed over several centuries, beginning as early earthworks in the 7th century BC. The most well-preserved sections, like the one pictured, were primarily built during the **Ming Dynasty (1368–1644)** to protect the empire against invasions from northern nomadic groups. It served as a military defense system, a border control point, and a means of regulating trade and migration along the Silk Road.

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Watchtowers:** Multi-story stone and brick structures used for housing soldiers, storing supplies, and signaling nearby units.
*   **Crenellated Parapets:** The notched tops of the walls provided cover for archers and soldiers.
*   **Battlements:** The defensive walls are constructed from kiln-fired bricks and stone blocks, often featuring a distinctive "waist" shape to follow the natural topography.
*   **Stairways and Ramps:** Integrated pathways that allow for the rapid movement of troops and equipment across steep, mountainous terrain.
*   **Curved Walls:** The structure follows the natural ridgelines of the mountains, demonstrating sophisticated engineering to maximize defensive visibility.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a massive fortification system built across the historical northern borders of China.

### History
The wall was constructed over several dynasties, beginning as early earthworks in the 7th century BC. The most well-preserved sections, like the one pictured, were primarily built during the **Ming Dynasty (1368–1644)**. Its primary purposes were to protect the Chinese empire against invasions from nomadic groups to the north, regulate trade along the Silk Road, and control immigration and emigration.

### Architectural Features
*   **Watchtowers:** Multi-story stone and brick structures used for housing soldiers, storing weapons, and signaling nearby units using smoke or fire.
*   **Battlements:** The notched parapets along the top of the wall provided cover for archers and soldiers.
*   **Curtain Walls:** The long, continuous stretches of wall connecting the towers, constructed from stone, brick, and rammed earth.
*   **Stairways and Ramps:** Integrated paths that follow the natural topography of the mountain ridges, allowing for the rapid movement of troops.
*   **Materials:** A combination of local stone, kiln-fired bricks, and lime mortar, which provided the durability seen in the Ming-era construction.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a massive fortification system built across the northern borders of China.

### History
The wall was constructed over several centuries, beginning as early earthworks during the Spring and Autumn period (770–476 BCE). The most famous sections were built during the **Ming Dynasty (1368–1644)**, which utilized brick and stone to create the durable structures seen today. Its primary purpose was to protect the Chinese empire against invasions from nomadic groups to the north, while also serving as a means of border control and the regulation of trade along the Silk Road.

### Visible Architectural Features
*   **Watchtowers:** Multi-story stone and brick structures used for housing soldiers, storing supplies, and signaling nearby units.
*   **Battlements:** The notched parapets along the top of the wall, designed to provide cover for archers and soldiers.
*   **Stairways and Ramps:** Steep, integrated stone steps and pathways that allow for the movement of troops and horses across rugged terrain.
*   **Curtain Walls:** Long, continuous stretches of masonry connecting the towers, built to follow the natural contours of the mountain ridges.
*   **Materials:** A combination of kiln-fired bricks, stone blocks, and rammed earth, often bonded with a traditional mortar made of lime and sticky rice.

Finish: stop

Correctness −2 and visual detail −3: sticky-rice mortar and rammed-earth composition are historical construction claims, not materials identifiable in this image. Clarity −3: the extended material/history explanation exceeds the concise request; the defensive history, Ming period, towers, parapets, and stairs are covered.

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a massive fortification system built across the historical northern borders of China.

### History
The wall was constructed over several centuries, beginning as early earthworks in the 7th century BC. The most well-preserved sections, like the one pictured, were primarily built during the **Ming Dynasty (1368–1644)**. Its primary purpose was to protect the Chinese empire against invasions from northern nomadic groups, while also serving as a means of border control, trade regulation, and communication.

### Visible Architectural Features
*   **Watchtowers:** Multi-story stone and brick structures used for housing soldiers, storing supplies, and providing a vantage point for surveillance.
*   **Crenellations:** The notched tops of the walls, designed to provide cover for archers and soldiers while allowing them to fire upon enemies.
*   **Battlements:** The parapets along the top of the wall that provide a walkway for rapid troop movement.
*   **Stairways and Ramps:** Integrated stone steps and sloping paths that allow the wall to follow the natural, rugged contours of the mountainous terrain.
*   **Materials:** A combination of kiln-fired bricks, stone blocks, and rammed earth, which were often bonded with a sticky rice mortar during the Ming era.

Finish: stop

Correctness −4 and visual detail −4: it guesses that the pictured stretch is Ming-built and presents rammed earth/sticky-rice mortar as visible material, neither of which can be established from the image. Clarity −4: battlements and crenellations repeat the parapet feature, and the long extra historical explanation is not concise.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a massive fortification system built across the northern borders of China.

**History:**
Construction began as early as the 7th century BC, with various dynasties contributing to its expansion and renovation. Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This is the **Great Wall of China**, a massive fortification system built across the historical northern borders of China.

### History
The wall was constructed over several centuries, beginning as early earthworks during the Spring and Autumn period (770–476 BC). The most famous sections were built during the **Ming Dynasty (1368–1644)**, which utilized brick and stone to create the durable structures seen today. Its primary purposes were to protect against invasions from northern nomadic groups, regulate trade along the Silk Road, and serve as a border control system.

### Visible Architectural Features
*   **Watchtowers:** Multi-story stone and brick towers used for housing soldiers, storing supplies, and signaling nearby units.
*   **Crenellated Parapets:** The notched tops of the walls provided cover for archers and soldiers.
*   **Battlements:** The high, thick defensive walls designed to withstand siege engines and projectiles.
*   **Stairways and Ramps:** Integrated stone steps and pathways that follow the natural topography of the mountain ridges.
*   **Materials:** A combination of kiln-fired bricks, stone blocks, and rammed earth, often bonded with a traditional mortar made of lime and sticky rice.

Finish: stop

Correctness −3 and visual detail −3: the lime/sticky-rice mortar is historical material information, not composition visible in the image. Clarity −4: the long Spring-and-Autumn/Ming history and repeated wall-feature list exceed the concise request.

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This is the Great Wall of China, a series of fortifications built along the northern borders of China to protect against invasions. The wall stretches over 13,000 miles and was constructed over several centuries, with the most famous sections dating from the Ming Dynasty (1368-1644).

Visible architectural features include:

- **Watchtowers**: These are used for observation and defense. They are typically made of brick and have small windows or openings for archers to shoot through.
- **Battlements**: These are the crenellated parapets along the top of the wall, designed to provide cover for defenders while allowing them to shoot arrows or fire weapons.
- **Staircases**: The wall has steep staircases that allow soldiers and civilians to climb up and down the wall.
- **Brickwork**: The wall is made of bricks, which were laid in a specific pattern to ensure stability and durability.

The Great Wall is a symbol of China's rich history and engineering prowess. It was built to protect the Chinese empire from invasions by nomadic tribes from the north. Today, it is a popular tourist destination and a UNESCO World Heritage Site.

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This is the Great Wall of China, a series of fortifications built across northern China. It was constructed to protect against invasions from the nomadic groups to the north. The wall is made of stone, brick, tamped earth, wood, and other materials, and is over 6,000 miles long.

Visible architectural features include watchtowers, which were used for military observation and communication, and battlements, which provided cover for soldiers. The wall also has a series of gates and passes, which were used to control access to the interior of the country.

The Great Wall was built in several stages, with the earliest sections dating back to the 7th century BC. The most famous section, the Badaling section, was built during the Ming Dynasty (1368-1644). The wall was completed in the 16th century, and has since been a popular tourist destination.

The Great Wall is a symbol of Chinese strength and resilience, and is one of the most iconic landmarks in the world. It is also a UNESCO World Heritage Site, and is recognized as one of the Seven Wonders of the Modern World.

Constructed over centuries, starting from the 7th century BC, the wall was primarily built to protect Chinese states and empires against invasions and raids by various nomadic groups from the Eurasian Steppe. The most famous sections were built during the Ming Dynasty (1368-1644).

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Watchtowers:** Several square towers with crenellated tops are visible, used for observation and defense.
*   **Battlements:** The top of the wall features a parapet with alternating high and low sections (merlons and crenels) for protection.
*   **Staircases:** Wide stone staircases allow access along the wall's path.
*   **Brickwork:** The wall is constructed from large, grey bricks.
*   **Masonry:** The base and lower sections of the wall often use stone or rammed earth.

ChineseWall · 89.00%

Identify this place and explain its history. Mention visible architectural features. Be concise and do not guess the exact section.

This is the Great Wall of China, a series of fortifications built across northern China.

**History:**
The Great Wall was constructed over many centuries, starting in the 7th century BC. It was originally built to protect Chinese states and empires from various nomadic groups from the north. The most famous sections were built during the Ming Dynasty (1368-1644) to defend against the Mongols.

**Visible Architectural Features:**
*   **Watchtowers:** Several square towers with tiled roofs are visible along the wall. These served as lookout points and defensive positions.
*   **Battlements:** The top of the wall features crenellations (merlons and crenels), which provided cover for defenders while allowing them to fire arrows or other projectiles.
*   **Stone Steps:** Wide stone staircases lead up to the wall and connect different sections.
*   **Brick and Stone Construction:** The wall is constructed from large blocks of stone and later brick, giving it a sturdy appearance.
*   **Parapet Walls:** The top of the wall has a low wall or parapet, providing additional protection for those walking along it.
*   **Gates and Passages:** There are openings in the wall that would have served as gates for controlled passage.

Finish: stop

Correctness −2 and visual detail −3: the claimed gates/passages are not clearly visible as gates in this stretch. Completeness −2: the historical context is brief and focused mainly on the Ming/Mongol period rather than the Wall’s multi-dynasty construction. Clarity −4: the six-item feature list is longer than needed.

Configurations: qwen35-MTP-Q4_K_M-vulkan-n2-mtp1-q4_0
